[trialAccount t18360613-1601]
1601. WILLIAM COLE, alias White , was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Joseph Ancony, on the 11th of May, at Barking, and stealing therein 1 coat, value 1 l.; 1 waistcoat, value 5 s., 1 pair breeches, value 15 s., 7 yard printed cotton, value 10 s.; 3 sheets, value 12 s.; 1 apron, value 1 s. 6 d.; 1 ear-ring, value 3 s.; 1 handkerchief, value 4 s.; and 4 shillings; his goods and monies—2nd COUNT, stating to be the dwelling-house of Robert Westley Hall Dare, Esq., since deceased.
JOSEPH ANCONY . I was lodge-keeper to the late Mr. Robert Westly Hall Dare, M. P.—he was living on the 11th of May in the parish of Barking. I left my house about eight o'clock that morning, and came to town with my family—we left all secure, and my wife locked the door in my presence—my wife returned about eight o'clock at night, and I returned about nine o'clock—I found my coat, waistcoat, and breeches gone, also a basket, 4 s. in money, seven yards of print, and three sheets—the house had been entered by breaking the window, and unlatching the fastening of the window—the prisoner lived in our neighbourhood.
MARY ANN ANCONY . I am the prosecutor's wife. I returned to the house about eight o'clock at night—I unlocked the door, and went in—I found the window broken, and opened—there was room for a man to put an arm in and open a latch—the things which were not taken were all left about the room, in a state of confusion—I missed the things my husband has named—I had seen them safe that morning.
CHARLES COLYER . I am a policeman. On the 11th of May last, about three o'clock in the afternoon, I apprehended the prisoner in Milend-road—he
was riding on one of the Stratford flies, coming towards London—he had a basket on the fly with him—it was given to me by the coachman, in his presence, who said it belonged to the prisoner—he did not say any thing to that—I found this coat, waistcoat, breeches, and clothes in the basket, and in his coat pocket this silk handkerchief and some money—I asked him how he came by the property—he said he had brought the whole of it from his brother, at Ilford—I took him to the police-office.
Prisoner. I told him I bought it for 25 s. Witness. When he was at the station-house he stated that he bought them at the Angel, at Stratford, of a man, for 25 s., but previous to that he said he brought them from his brother, at Ilford.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY .* Aged 19.— Transported for Life.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18360613-1602]
1602. HENRY GREEN EATON was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of May, at West Ham, 5 handkerchiefs, value 5 s., the goods of Martha Pratt; 1 watch value 1 l.; 1 watch-ribbon, value 1 s.; 1 watch-key, value 1 d.; 4 spoons, value 6 s.; 4 sovereigns; 1 £10 and 1£5 Banknote; the goods and monies of Susanna Maria Eaton, in her dwelling-house.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
SUSANNA MARIA EATON . I live at Stratford-green, West Ham. I have a lease of the house—I am single—the prisoner is my brother's son—he was in the habit of spending part of his vacation at my house, but I had forbidden him the house latterly—on Sunday, the 15th of May, I left my house to go to church, and when I returned, after the morning service, my servant opened the door—in consequence of what she said I went upstairs and found mu watch gone from the stand on my bed-room mantel-piece—I went to my closet to look for some money which I had put in the evening before, and found a £10 and a £5 Bank-note, four sovereigns, and some silver gone, and an old purse taken out of a pocket with 2 s. or 3 s.—I missed four silver tea-spoons from a closet in the front room, and other things—I went with his father in search of him that evening, and found him at Wright's Coffee-house, Charing-cross—I said, "Henry, you have been and robbed me again"—he said, "I have not been your house"—I said, "Yes, you have, and you have taken a great deal now; you have taken a great deal of money, and you have taken the watch again"—he said, "Ah, I have done it; you have put a halter round my neck"—I had a policeman with me—he said I should have all the things again—he did not say what things—as he went along he wanted to get his arm loose, but he did not—at the police-office they were going to search him, and he said, "Oh, here, I will give up the watch; here is the watch, and that is all you will find"—it is mine—it was not in a case—the case was gone—it had not been in it the stand, but he produced it in the case—the watch and case had not been together when I left them—he said at the station-house that his father could not afford to give him any thing, as he had got a family, and as I would not, he must have it somehow—when the policeman went to search him for the notes, he said, "Oh, you need not trouble yourself to search me, they are in too safe hands for you to get at them"
MARTHA PRATT . I occupy two apartments in the prosecutrix's house. On the day in question I went her to church—I left my room with the
drawers all shut—the bed-room window was open—when I returned I found my room in very great confusion, mu drawers open and the window-shutter quite open—it had been rather closed—I missed five pocket-handkerchief and a large carving-knife and case with it, from the bureau—the officer found my two handkerchiefs on the prisoner—these are mine.
WILLIAM EVE ( police-constable A 122.) On the evening of the 15th of May I received the prisoner in charge—when I got to the station-house, he said, "All you will find about me belongs to my aunt—I took the watch, and here it is"—he gave it to me after the inspector took the charge, and I searched him afterwards—I took two handkerchiefs from him—I told him I was searching him for a £10 and a £5 note—he said, "It is no use your searching me now; they are in too safe hands for you to get them".
SUSANNA MARIA EATON re-examined. This is my watch and case—it has a gold face, but a metal case.
ANN HUGHES I am servant to Miss Eaton. On the 15th of May my mistress and Mrs. Pratt went out to church—after they were gone I saw the watch on the mantel-piece in her room—I did not see the prisoner at all, or hear him—nobody could get into the house except at Mrs. Pratt's bed-room window, which was wide open when I got the room—a person might get to that window from the garden.
Prisoner's Defence. Some time ago I was committed to prison for want of bail—I became acquainted with a young man of worse character than—he worked out of me the secrets of my aunt—I had perhaps robbed her before, but it was through distress—this young man worked out that secret—he told me it was his intention to go down there and rod her—I told him if he did I certainly should give him into custody—I did not see him for a few weeks afterwards, till the Sunday this took place, when I met him in Drury-lane—he informed me that he had been to my aunt, but not that he had robbed her—he asked me to take something to drink—I remember hearing him tell me the watch might discover me—it was not a suitable place to give in charge, knowing he was a desperate character, and not being able to defend myself, I might have got into more trouble than I have now, if I had.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Life.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18360613-1603]
1603. JAMES SAGE and JONATHAN SURRIDGE were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of May, 1 colt skin, value 18 d., the goods of William Burrell.
WILLIAM BURRELL . I live at Chingford-green, and am a farmer. I lost the skin of a colt in May from the shed in the farm-yard—I went away on the Sunday morning from home, and when I came back it was gone—I saw it at Waltham Abbey, at Mr. Clark's a tanner's I think, on the 21st—I had seen it safe at home on the 17th, in a cow-shed, in the farm-yard—the prisoners have both worked for me—Sage had worked for me about six days before.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long had the skin been hanging there? A. Four or five days—I saw it on Sunday, and on Thursday I saw it again—it was in a very good state to swear to—there were very good marks on it—it was brown—part of the nose was cut—it
was not cut in any particular way—I did not skin the colt myself, a man did, who is not here—I had examined it when it was in the shed, so as to be able to swear to it—it was cut right across the nose, which is not the ordinary way to cut it—it was my colt skin—I had buried the colt—the skin was not compared with the colt.
COURT. Q. Was it a colt of yours? A. Yes, one I bred myself—I have no doubt but that it was my colt's skin—the prisoners both knew my premises very well—this skin hung up to dry.
GEORGE CLARK . I live at Waltham Abbey; my father is a tanner. On the 18th of May I saw both the prisoners at my father's tan-yard—they brought a colt's skin—I brought it—they came together—I paid them 1 s. 6 d. for it—Mr. Burrell's man came the same night—I saw the same skin produced before the Magistrate, and the prosecutor swore to it.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you buy it? A. Yes—it was brought in a sack—I did not ask how they came by it—there was nothing suspicious about it—it was very stale—1 s. 6 d. was the full value of it—if it had been in a good state, it would have been worth 4 s. or 5 s.—it was not fit to make leather.
JOHN PEARSON . I was throwing up dung in Mr. Burrell's yard, and the skin was in the shed in the yard when I left work, at six o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Do you know the two prisoners? A. Yes—they have always borne good characters.
JOHN SMALL . I am a constable. I took Sage—I was taking him to prison, and he said they were both in it—he did not say how they got the skin.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. How long have you known them? A. Ten Years—I never knew any thing against them.
SAGE— GUILTY . Aged 17.
SURRIDGE— GUILTY . Aged 25.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18360613-1604]
1604. THOMAS WHITTON and GEORGE PHILLIPS were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of May, 5 yards of printed cotton, value 3 s. 9 d., the goods of William Feary.
RACHEL FEARY . I am the wife of William Feary; we keep a shop at West Ham, in Essex. On the 12th of May, between one and two o'clock, I had put this print in my window—I went up-stairs, and had not been gone above three minutes when my daughter called me down—I came down, and the print was gone—I went out of the shop—I did not see any one—a boy showed me where they were gone to—I went to the Marsh-gate—I saw the two prisoners in the act of doubling up my print in a handkerchief—I took it from them, and said, "You villains, you have stolen this from me"—they then threw two more parcels down—I took up my own, and called "Stop thief" and two witnesses, now here, caught them and took them to the station-house—I am sure they are the same—they were not out of my sight till they were taken—this is my print—this handkerchief belongs to the prisoners—here are five yards and a half of cotton of mine—I had not seen the prisoners near my shop—they were a hundred yards from the shop when I first saw them.
RICHARD WARD . I was having a pint of beer, and heard the lady sing out "Stop thief"—I stopped Whitton.
JOHN MERRY . I heard the cry of "Stop thief," and pursued Phillips, and took him to the station-house.
PHINEAS BUTLER ( police-constable K 222). I was at the station-house,
and the prisoners were brought there with this property, of the prosecutor's, and these other two parcels.
Whitton. We saw the bundles on the ground in Marsh-gate-lane, and picked them up, but did not go away with them.
(John Green, a shoemaker, of Parliament-court, Artillery-lane; and Richard Cas, deposed to Phillip's good character; and W. Holmes, a shoe-manufacturer, to that of Whitton's)
WHITTON— GUILTY . Aged 13.
PHILLIPS— GUILTY . Aged 13.
Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18360613-1605]
1605. CATHERINE KELL was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 26 Yards of linen cloth, value 1 l., 13 s., the goods of Aaron Gregory
AARON GREGORY . I am a linen-draper, living at Woolwich, On the 16th of May, between two and three o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came into my shop and asked me for some striped shirting—while I turned my back to reach some down, I observed her take a piece of linen from the counter, and conceal it in her apron—she had a shawl on—she objected to the pattern of the shirting—I then reached her down another piece—she said that would do, and ordered me to cut her off half-a-yard, which came to 5 1/4 d.—I wrapped the half-yard up in paper—she gave me 6 d.—I gave her change and she left—I followed and took hold of her, and said she had stolen a piece of linen—she had not got five steps from the door—she said she had not—I took hold of her, shoved her inside the door, and saw her drop it—I took it up, and at the same time a print dress dropped from her apron, but that did not belong to me.
Prisoner. I was never outside the door. Witness. Yes, she went to the window and spoke to two or three women there.
Prisoner. He pushed me into the room, and pushed me down in a chair—I asked him to allow me to stand up and loosen my stays, and he said if I did not sit down he would shake my guts out. Witness. I did not—she told my good lady, that she had got the cholic, and began to loosen her stays—she undid her clothes to her stays—I thought she would quite undress herself.
WILLIAM CAMPION . I am a constable. The prisoner was given into my custody—I searched and found this half-yard of calico, and 13 s. 9 d. on her, which I gave to her husband by direction of the Magistrate, and three dupilcates.
Prisoner. The piece of print I had just taken from pledge, and had it in my apron—I only beg pardon—I was guilty of the crime.
GUILTY . Aged 41.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18360613-1606]
1606. DANIEL EVANS was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 1 pillow-case, value 6 d., 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.; 2 combs, value 6 d.; half a yard of ribbon, value 3 d.; and 1 pair of stockings, value 6 d.; the goods of Nicholas Stanton.
ELIZABETH STANTON . I live servant at the Lord Whitworth, at Woolwich. I am the wife of Nicholas Station—he is alive, but does not live with me—on the 26th of May I saw the prisoner on the stairs of the public-house, putting my things into his pocket—I asked him what business he had with them—he said he had no business with them, and he did not intend to take them away—I had left them safe on the bad in the room
that he entered—I did not see him enter the room—I do not occupy it, but my boxes were there—the prisoner was on the first step coming down stairs—I took a pillow-case, a pair of combs, a handkerchief, and a piece of ribbon from him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Had you seen him in the house that day? A. Yes; his brother was there also—I never saw them before—these things are not worth above 1 s. or 2 s.—I did not see him leave any tools with my mistress, but there were some there—he was in a state of drunkenness—if he had taken away these things he could not have come back for his tools—they were more valuable than the things—he was dunk when he came, and he drank a little in our house—he was not shown into that room to sleep—he came out of the room he was shewn into, and went to the other—they are close to one another—he was to have a bed in the house, being so drunk.
COURT. Q. Do you think he was so drunk that he might have taken them without an intent to steal them? A. I do not know that he did know what he was doing—he had nearly got them all into his pocket, but I saw my handkerchief—he took all the things that were loose, of a small description.
MARY ANN WATKINS . I live in Charles-street, Woolwich. I was at the Lord Whitworth that night—I showed the prisoner up to a room, as the servant was cleaning herself—the mistress asked me to show him the room to go to bed—I came down directly after—he appeared to be tipsy—he did not seem merry—he said he wished to go to bed as he was very tired.
Cross-examined. Q. Is the landlady here? A. No; I saw a parcel, but do not know what it contained—it was delivered into the constable's hands.
WILLIAM BEADLE I am a watchman. The prisoner was given into my custody at the Lord Whitworth—I asked him if he had any money—he said no, he had not—I asked if he had paid for his lodgings—he said, "No"—I asked him if he knew what he had been doing—he said no, but if he had done wrong he was very sorry for it—I produce the things.
Cross-examined. Q. Was not there at least five times the value of these things in tools? A. Yes; they were given up to his brother.
NOT GUILTY .
Second Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18360613-1607]
1607. JOHN MYERS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th May, 1 pencil-case, value 5 s.; and 1 memorandum book, value 6 d.; the goods of Patrick Bird, from his person.
PATRICK BIRD . I am a wine porter, and live in Knight Ryder-street, Doctor's Commons. On the 24th of May, I was at Greenwich Fair, about three o'clock, and lost from my pocket a pencil case and memorandum book—these are the articles—( looking at them)—they were produced to me by the officer.
THOMAS HOBBS ( police-constable C 85.)I was at Greenwich Fair on the 24th of May, about three o'clock in the day, and saw the prisoner there—I saw him go behind the prosecutor, put his hand into his coat-pocket, and take this book and put it into the flap of his trowsers—I took hold of him—found the property on him, and took him to the station-house.
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined One Month and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18360613-1608]
1608. ROBERT BELL was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May, 1 purse, value 4 d.; 2 half-crowns; and 1 shilling; the goods and monies of William Tytherly, from the person of Susannah Tytherl.
SUSANNAH TYTHERLY . I am the wife of William Tytherly, who is employed in the surgery at King's College—we live in Pitt-street, Fitzory-street. On the 23rd of May I was at Greenwich Fair, and lost my purse, containing two half-crowns, and one shilling—I think it was between six and seven o'clock in the evening, but I am not exactly certain of the time—I was walking with my husband, and coming through the fair, leaning on his arm—I felt somebody annoying me through the fair, and saw three young men behind me—I thought they were countrymen and did it more from ignorance than any thing—when we stopped they stopped—soon after we got into a terrible crowd, I felt a pressure and looked round—I saw the same three young men, and said to my husband, "These chaps annoy me so I cannot put up with it any longer"—he said, "They have not picked you pocket have they?"—I felt, and said my purse was gone—he run after them—the prisoner was one of them—I saw no more of my husband till he came home—my purse has since been produced by the officer.
WILLIAM TYTHERLY . I am the prosecutrix's husband. I was with her in the fair on the 23rd of May—I took hold of the prisoner on suspicion of robbing her—I said to him, "Give me up the purse you have taken from my wife"—he said, "What purse?" and made a hit at me my jaw, but did not hit me—I said, "The purse you have in your pocket"—he said, "I have no purse"—I instantly put my hand into his left pocket and took it out, and said, "This is my purse, "—he said, "For God's sake let me go"—I had seen him and the other two trying to pick gentleman's pockets in the fair before, which made me notice them—there were two half-crowns and one shilling in the purse as there in now.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I had not been in the fair ten minutes—I picked the purse up.
GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18360613-1609]
1609. REBECCA ANN MACE was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, 2 decanters, value 8 s., the goods of Henry Dunford; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
HENRY DUNFORD . I am a broker, and live in Church-street, Greenwich On the 14th of May, at nine o'clock in the morning, I saw these decanters, with four more standing on a chest of drawers in the shop, and at four o'clock in the afternoon they were missing.
JOSEPH CUNNINGHAM . I am shopman to Mr. Parry, a pawnbroker in Flagon-row, Deptford, It is about half-a-mile from Greenwich—I produce two decanters, which were pawned by the prisoner on the 14th of May, in the name of Elizabeth Brown, for 3 s.—I do not know the time—it was after two o'clock in the afternoon I believe—I cannot say whether it was before four o'clock.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
LUKE FEENEY ( police-constable R. 82.)I got this certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, from Mr. Clark's officer—I was present when the prisoner was tried here in February last—I was a witness in the case—she is the person mentioned in the certificate ( read)
Prisoner's Defence. I own I pawned them, but I am not the person who stole them, not do I know much of the person—she came from Gosport, and was at the Grapes in Friday-street—she told me she would give me 1 s.
to pawn them for 4 s., but I only got 3 s.,—she said she wanted to go to Lewisham, and was going to Gosport again.
JOHN HOILE . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner—she did not make this statement then, or refer at all to this—I took her for some prints.
GUILTY . Aged 30.
[trialAccount t18360613-1610]
1610. REBECCA ANN MACE was again indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 21 yards of printed cotton, value 10 s., the goods of John Breakspear, and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN WALLINGTON . I am shopman to Mr. John Breakspear, a linen-draper, in London-street, Greenwich. On Friday, the 20th May, about eight o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner come to the shop-door, but she did not come in—she took the print, put it under her shawl, and walked away with it—I went after her, and asked her where she was going with that piece of print—she said she had purchased it in Holborn—I gave her to the officer—the print has a private mark on it.
Prisoner. He says I had it under my shawl—he took it up in the doorway—I was not off the threshold, when he tapped me on the shoulder. Witness. She was twenty yards from the door, carrying it under her shawl—I did not tell a policeman if he did not take her I would fetch one that would.
JOHN HOILE . I took the prisoner into custody at the prosecutor's door—she denied having taken the print.
LUKE FEENEY . I produced the certificate as in the former case.
GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18360613-1611]
1611. ELIZABETH JANE WALES was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of May, in the dwelling-house of Ann Parrott, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s.6 d.; 1 petticoat, value 1 s.; 2 sovereigns, 1 £10 and 1£5 Bank-note, her property; she having been before convicted of felony; and WILLIAM PARTRIDGE was indicted for feloniously receiving the said £10 Bank-note, well knowing it to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c.
ANN PARROTT . I am a widow. My dwelling-house is in the Woolwich-road near the Hare and Billets in the perish of Greenwich—I go out charing, and keep fowls and pigs—I let lodgings sometimes—I had not been acquainted with Wales more than five days before this happened—she was entirely a stranger before I first saw her, which was on a Monday in May, and I missed my property on Saturday, the 14th—the ostler at the Hare and Billet came to me about half-past nine o'clock in the evening, and asked me if I could let her have a night's lodging—she came with him—he said she had no money, but what charge I made charge I made he would pay, which he did—it was 4 d. for the night's lodging—she slept there on that night, and the next morning she left to go to Gravesend, as she said, to receive some money—I believe she was gone three or four days—she returned again and came about half-past nine o'clock at night, (I think it was on Tuesday,) and said would I let her have a lodging—she remained with me till the Saturday—on the Thursday I went out to cook a dinner, and I left her in my house—on the Friday I went out to clear up what was left and returned—I found nothing a miss till the Saturday night—I was
at home all that day doing my work, and the prisoner was in the house with me—I had not looked to see whether my property was safe on the Friday or Saturday—it might have been taken away on the Friday—I kept it in a chest, down stairs—I had seen it was right on the Thursday—it was in a pocket-book, in a small chest which was locked—on the Saturday evening I discovered my loss, I went up to Greenwich on an errand about seven o'clock, and left the prisoner in the house—as I was returning I met her about half or a quarter of a mile from the house—she said she was going to William Partridge to carry his provisions and some things—I had seen him twice at my house—on the Friday evening he was there—she had got a basket and she said she was to see him at eight o'clock, and she would be back by nine o'clock—she did not come by ten o'clock, and I waited till near eleven o'clock—I was then going to bed, and found the bad-room door locked—there was no reason for her not returning that I know of—I could not find the key—she slept in the same room with me—part of the property was down stairs in the chest, and the handkerchief was in the bed-room—I was obliged to break my bed-room door open, and then I found the drawer open—the key of the bed-room was put in a place where I did not find it for a day or two after—I missed a handkerchief from the drawer, and them I found the keys and came down, and found my chest all tumbled about—I missed from my pocket-book one £5 and one £10 note, and two sovereigns—I went to see about it, and met the policeman—he went in search of her—I never permitted her to take or property—she was without money, and clothing almost—she had no money to pay her lodgings—I gave her her breakfast—she was shabby and poor in appearance, and she told me she was destitute of money—she had a change of linen from me.
Prisoner Wales. Q. Did you lend me any linen to put on my person? A. Yes—I never authorized you to pledge a petticoat and a handkerchief.
Q. Did you not tell me you were out of money, and I was to write you a petition to give to the member of Parliament? A. No—I had to set up a son in business, and a person told me if I was to draw a petition I might get some money.
COURT. Q. Did you lend her 2 l.? A. Never.
RICHARD IRWIN . I keep the Sun Tavern, in Shooter's-hill Road I paid the prosecutrix a £10 note on Friday, the 20th of March—this is the same note—I received it from Masters and Co. on the 25th of March—here is my name on it, which I marked at the time ( looking at it).
JOHN RICHARD WILLIAMS . I am a licensed victualler. The priosner Wales, came on the 14th of May, to my house, and called for glass of shrub and brandy—Mrs. Williams served her—she then called for a pint of half-and-half—there was a waterman with her—she gave him the half-and-half, and drank the shrub and brandyshe tendered a sovereign to Mrs. Williams, who went and got the change in the tap-room, and gave it to her—she asked the waterman to give her a piece of paper, which he did—she put it on the counter, and put the silver into it, and then she took a parcel, either out of her bosom or pocket—she turned it over the silver, and I saw it was a £10 note, but by the bulk of it I think there was more than one, but I can swear to a £10 note.
Wales. He never saw the note opened.
JOHN ADAMAN ( police-sergeant R 1) received information from
the prosecutrix—I mad inquiry—I went on board the Dreadnought, and took the male prisoner into custody—he is carpenter's mate on board, but I found nothing on him but 3 s. 6 d.—he was in the cage on Sunday, and on Monday I received further information that the money was sewn up in his pocket—I went, and found he had got an extra jacket, and I cut this £10 not out of the cuff of this jackets, which I have here.
POSTHUMOUS MENSE ( police-constable R 195) On Monday, the 16th of May, I received information that the female prisoner was drinking at the Unicorn public-house, in Horse Ferry-road—I went and took her, and on her I found a duplicate of the petticoat and silk handkerchief—she took the duplicate out of her pocket, and I took it immediately.
EDWARD CARTER . I am a pawnbroker at Greenwich. I have a petticoat and a handkerchief which were pawned by the female prisoner in the name of Wales.
ANN PARROTT re-examined. This is my handkerchief and my petticoat—I never permitted her to pawn them, or to take any thing—I took her in from charity—she said she was a parson's daughter.
CHARLES NICHOLLS ( police-constable R 164) I produce a certificate of the prisoner Wales's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office ( read)—she is the person.
JOHN ADAMAN re-examined. When I went on board the Dreadnought, I asked Partridge if he had been on shore that night—he said, "Yes"—I said, had he been in company with a female—he said, yes a woman he cohabited with, but he wished he could get rid of her—I asked if he had got any money—he said, no, she had given him no money whatever—he said he had sent her 10 s. to get what she wanted—he said she gave him no money or notes.
Wales's Defence. This man has been my only protector for twelve months—I thought to go to my parish, but they would not do any thing for me—I determined to go there—I received 1 l. 0 s. 4 d. in silver, and then I met a man who gave me a sovereign for the silver—I walked as far as Greenwich on this night, and the ostler took me to Mrs. Parrott's—in the morning I got up, and told her I was to go to Gravesend again by order of the overseers—I returned on the Tuesday, and the man and she were drinking some beer together—she said she had to go to Woodwich, and could not see me till seven o'clock in the evening—she came home and took me in again, and on the Wednesday, she said she was going to cook a dinner—she left me 2 1/2., and said if I wanted any thing I might pledge her handkerchief and petticoat; and in the evening a respectable man passed, and beckoned to me—he gave me two half-crowns, and appointed me to meet him again, which I did—he told me I should never want for any thing any more and gave me a note and told me it was a £2 note—the male prisoner came on shore, and said he would send me his money to get his things—the person told me at the shop that if I stopped at the prosecutrix house, she would say I had robbed her, as she had been un Newgate with a person not long before—I said she could not lose by me, because she was very poor—I then went to Partridge, and gave him the note and two shillings, and he did not know how I came by it—the prosecutrix desired me before to write to Mr. Angerstein to get some money—she said she would go and take a lodging at Woolwich, where there were many officers, as I was a good-looking woman we should do very well.
William Partridge's Defence. The woman gave me the note, and as I did not know where she got it, I sewed it up.
ANN PARROTT re-examined. The £10 note I lost was the one I received from Mr. Irwin.
WALES— GUILTY . Aged 35.— Transported for Life.
PARTRIDGE— GUILTY .— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined for One Year.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.

1612. HENRY FREEMAN and JOHN NEEHAN were indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of May, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of David Bean, from his person; and that Neehan had been before convicted of felony.
DAVID BEAN . On the 23rd of May, I was at Greenwich fair, and had my pocket picked—I did not feel it done, or see any body—Dyke told me of it, and produced my handkerchief.
WILLIAM DYKE . I am a policeman. I attended the fair—I observed the two prisoners following the prosecutor up and down the fair—I saw Freeman go behind him and take the handkerchief from his pocket—Neehan was close behind him, covering him—I immediately took them into custody, and took the handkerchief from Freeman's hand, as he drew it from the pocket—I secured them both—I had seen them together for nearly an hour—in company and acting together—I searched them—I found nothing but a pair of kid gloves and a knife on Neehan, and 2 d. on Freeman—it was four or five o'clock in the afternoon.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
JAMES HAYWOOD . I am a policeman. I have a certificate of the conviction of Neehan, which I received at Mr. Clark's office ( read)—I was present at the trail—he is the person who was convicted.
Freeman's Defence. We had not been in the fair a quarter of an hour before we were taken.
Neehan's Defence. I was standing by at the time the policeman laid hold of him, he laid hold of me afterwards, and said we had been picking pockets together.
FREEMAN— GUILTY . Aged 15.
NEEHAN— GUILTY .* Aged 14.
Transported for Life.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360613-1613]
1613. ELIZA TIPPING was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of June, 15 yards of silk, value 32 s., the goods of Thomas Phillips and others,
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS PHILLIPS . I am silk manufacturer and have two partners, we live in Fort-street, Old Artillery Ground. I employed the prisoner and her husband at times—on the 3rd of June I gave her husband 110 bobbins of shuts, weighing 18lbs. 7ozs. or raw silk, to manufacture—on the Thursday morning, the 16th, I went to the prisoner's house, and saw a loom with a portion of silk left in it, and brought this part of it away—I cut it off myself—I had advanced 1 l. odd on the work—the silk was to be manufactured for me, and the whole together would have made 103 yards—it was the man I employed—this 15 yards of silk produced by the pawnbroker corresponds with the silk I brought
from the loom, and with a note which has been made by the seissors in cutting it out—it corresponds in quality, and in every respect—I value it at 32 s.
MARY ANN ARTIS . I am the wife of a Greenwich pensioner. The prisoner and her husband live in East-street, Greenwich—I occupy was apartment under them—the husband was employed occasionally in weaving—I am acquainted with the noise the loom makes, as I have been in the business myself—on Tuesday morning, the 14th, between ten and eleven o'clock I heard the roll going—that was the roller used in the loom—the work is upon it—the silk is put on it—I heard it unrolled and rolled again, and said something to my husband about it.
MARY TIPPING I am the prisoner's son. On the 14th of June I went out of the room at eight o'clock in the morning, and there was this silk then on the roll—there was a part remaining after it had been cut off—my father did not come home that morning—I returned about seven o'clock in the evening, and something was said to me by Mr. Artis—in consequence of that, I went to the loom—Mr. Artis had seen me at two o'clock that afternoon at the East-gate—the moment I went home I went to the loom, and found it rolled up in a very rough manner—I found a paste-board on the top of it, to cover it so that nobody could suppose it had been touched—I took the paste-board away next morning, and found the silk was gone—I unrolled it myself in the morning and found the end of it as it is now—we could see that it was cut—I could not tell how much was gone—my mother was present—I said to her, "Mother, you have done a pretty job for yourself"—she said, "Well, I must answer for it myself"—the policeman took her into custody.
CHARLES WILLIAMS . I am a pawnbroker at Greenwich. I produce 15 3/4 yards of silk, which was pledged by the prisoner, on Tuesday morning, the 14th of June, about twelve o'clock, for 12 s.
DONALD STEWART ( police-constable R 35.) I took the prisoner into custody on the 15th—I heard what her soon stated to her, as he has stated to-day—I asked he how she came to do so—she said she did it on purpose to get rid of her husband, making use of a bad expression, and pointing to him at the time—when I took her to the station-house she said, if the duplicate was not found, they could make nothing of her—she then said she was sure Mr. Phillips would not prosecute, on account that the prosecution would fall on himself.
GUILTY . Aged 46.— Confined Nine Months.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

WILLIAM CHARTRES . I am assistant-surgeon in the Royal Military Hospital. The prisoner is the wife of a marine, and was employed to wait on my wife and family—on Saturday morning, the 4th of June, I missed my table-cloth—I went to the prisoner's house, called a constable, and her husband produced the pawnbroker's ticket of a table-cloth, a flannel shirt, and coral necklace—this is the property.
THOMAS PACEY BIRTS . I am a pawnbroker. I produce all these articles—the shirt was pledged on the 20th of May; on the 1st of June,
the table-cloth; and on the 3rd of June, this necklace; all by the prisoner.
GUILTY . Aged 30— Confined Three Months.

1615. CHARLES WILSON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of May, 1 shilling, 1 sixpence, 1 halfpenny, and 1 farthing, the monies of Elizabeth Crow, from her person.
ELIZABETH CROW . I am single, and live in Long-walk, Bermondsey-square. On Tuesday evening, between five and six o'clock, I was at Greenwich fair with my sister—I felt a hand in my pocket—I turned, and the officer had got the prisoner—I had a shilling, a sixpence, a halfpenny, and a farthing—my money was gone, and his hand was taken as it came out of my pocket—the money fell on the ground.
GEORGE POWELL . ( police-constable E 10.) I saw the prisoner with another—I saw the prisoner put this hand into the prosecutrix's pocket—I seized it, and drew out one shilling, one sixpence, one half-penny, and one farthing—he dropped it—I took him to the station-house—he said he should not have done it but for the other boy.
Prisoner She said she would not appear against me, if my father gave her 5 l
ELIZABETH CROW . I saw his father on Sunday—I said I would not say more than I could help, but nothing, was said about 5 l
GUILTY .* Aged 13— Transported for Seven Years.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

1616. JOHN CONLAY . was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of May, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of John Hagell, from his person.
GEORGE VICLERS ( police-constable L 54.) I was in Greenwich park on the 23rd of May, and saw the prisoner in company with another person—I saw the prisoner try several pockets, and then take this handkerchief from the prosecutor's—I took him with it.
JOHN HAGELL . I was in the park, and lost this handkerchief from my pocket—I did not feel it taken—the offer spoke to me, and I saw the prisoner near me.
Prisoner I saw the handkerchief at the gentleman's feet and the officer said I took it.

[trialAccount t18360613-1617]
1617. JOHN LONG was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 1 coat, value 2 l., the goods of James Fagan.
JAMES FAGAN . I am a painter, and live at Royal-hill, Greenwich. On the 21st of May, I was in the Eight Bells there—I had been in the house but two or three minutes—I went in between two and three o'clock in the morning—a man challenged me to fight—he broke the glass I had with some bran day and water in it—there was no place to sit down—I took off my coat and lost it, but I do not know who took it—I put it on the counter of the bar—we did not fight—the man ran away, and I ran after him—it was not the prisoner but another man that wanted to fight me—I missed the coat before he ran away, and I suspected he was connected with the parties who were there—there were seven or eight persons standing together when I went in—they appeared to be conversing about different subjects.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How many persons were there altogether? A. About six or eight—I was passing from the fair with a friend—I was with the superintendent of the police and the high-constable, but they were not there then—I went in with a Mr. Tuck—he is not here—I had not been into any other public-house the whole day—I was engaged in my business till half-past ten o'clock—I had been at Mr. Tuck's, and drank six pennyworth of warm brandy and water—I do not say the prisoner was at the public-house—I never saw him till he was in custody—he might have been behind me.
JOHN MARTIN MEWS . I am a pensioner in the Royal Hospital, and I sell coffee in the street. At a quarter past three o'clock, on the 26th of May, I was selling coffee about three yards from the door of the Bells—I saw the prisoner come out with a coat under his right arm—he went about two yards—there was no body with him—he took and shook it, and folded it up, and clapped it under his left arm—he went round by the church—it was quite light, I could see the dial—he is the man.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you sure you saw the dial? A. Yes—there are four to the church—it was a quarter after three o'clock—I had drank nothing but coffee—I arrived there at ten o'clock at night—I had had nothing but a glass of peppermint—I had been to no public-house that morning.
JOHN SIZER . ( police-constable R 103.) On Thursday morning, at twenty minutes past three o'clock, Mr. Fagan told me he had lost his coat, and Mews told me something—I went after the prisoner, and found him near Greenwich church about twenty minutes past three—I asked if he had got a coat belonging to any one besides himself—he said, no, he had not—I did not search him—I asked him a second time, and he again denied it—I then said I should take him into custody, on suspicion of having stolen one—after we had walked four or five paces, he said he had a coat in the boot of his coach—we turned back again towards the coach, and he pulled this coat out from the boot of an omnibus coach, and gave it to me—this is it—I do not know whether he was the driver of it.
Cross-examined Q. Did he appear sober? A. He might have had some drink, but I did not see any effects of liquor.
Prisoner's Defence I picked the coat up on the step of the door.
EDWARD SMITH . I know the prisoner—he is foreman of Mr. Wolfe's omnibuses—I was with him that morning, and we had a pint of half-and-half together—I saw a fight with the prosecutor and another person—the prisoner left the house first, and I followed him—there were about ten person in front of the bar—when the prisoner got out I saw him shake this coat—he said, "I have got a coat of Somebody's—I picked it up on the threshold of the landlord's door"—it was all over dust—it had been trodden on in the room—he said, "I am going to put it into the boot of the omnibus, and when the owner comes he shall have it."
ALFRED WOLFE . I am the prisoner master. I was standing minding the omnibuses, and I saw the witness, Mews, at this time—I believe him to be drunk—the prisoner told me he had a cost placed in the boot of the omnibus.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18360613-1618]
1618. JOHN ADAMS and WILLIAM REDRUP were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 5 s., the goods of James Taverner, from his person.
JAMES TAVERNER . I live at Chelmsford. On Sunday, the 25th of
May, I was at Greenwich fair, and missed my handkerchief from my pocket, in the fair—it was produced by the officer—this is it.
JAMES ANDREWS ( police constable M 45) I was on duty there, on the 25th of May—I saw the prisoners, and watched them a quarter of an hour, in company with a third person—I saw them attempt several persons' pocket, at last they came to the prosecutor, and Redrup took from his pocket this handkerchief, and gave it to Adams, who threw it down—I seized them both and took them to the station-house—I found on Redrup, a snuff-box and a pair of scissors.

[trialAccount t18360613-1619]
1619. CHARLES COOPER was indicted for stealing, on the 23rd of May, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d., the goods of George Means, from his person.
GEORGE MEANS . I am a tailor, and live at Ann's-place, Hoxton. On the 23rd of May, I was at Greenwich fair, between the hours of three and four o'clock in the afternoon—Wild, the officer, tapped me on the shoulder and told me something—I immediately turned round, and at two or three yards distance, I saw the handkerchief lying on the ground—Wild picked it up, and collared the prisoner, and Dyke (another office) collared another person, and they were taken to the station-house—this is my handkerchief,
JAMES WILD ( police-constable R 141.) I was on duty on the 23rd of May, at Greenwich fair—I saw the prosecutor there, and the prisoner and another young man were attempting several gentleman's pockets, going up and down the fair—another was with them, smoking a pipe—I then saw the prisoner and the other two men go behind the prosecutor—the prisoner took the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket, and put it into his trowsers pocket—he then pushed away from the prosecutor, and turned back to the fair—I torched the prosecutor, and told him—I then laid hold of the prisoner—he took and threw the handkerchief behind on the ground—I picket it up myself and took him.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Do you understand your duty in a place of this kind? A. Yes, it is to prevent crime—we did not take them till we satisfied ourselves what they were about—I saw them attempt several pockets—we have had an order before, from the superintendent's office. at Greenwhich, not to take attempts—I cannot give you any name, but I know I have received that order—the order was given at the office three or four years ago—Grant is the name of the other boy who attempted to pick pockets—the bills been ignored against him, and the third boy also.
WILLIAM DYKE ( police-constable R 193.) I was in company with Wild—I saw the prisoner at the fair, and two more—I saw Cooper take the handkerchief from the prosecutor's pocket—I saw it come from the pocket—Wild was before me—he took him into custody—Wild caught hold of Copper directly—I saw the handkerchief on the ground—I took Grant and the other prisoner.
Cross-examined Q. How near you to the prisoner? A. Not more than one yard—Wild and the other two were between me and the prisoner—I saw the handkerchief in his hand—Wild immediately caught hold of him—I did not see him throw it on the ground—he might
have dropped the handkerchief directly—I did not see him put it into any pocket, or attempt to do so—I was in plain clothes, and so was Wild—I was not called the Grand Jury, although I was with him—no one but the prosecutor and Wild were called in.
COURT. Q. You saw the handkerchief in the prisoner's hand? A. I did—I then immediately took the other two—my attention was called off to them and I did not see the handkerchief on the ground.
Prisoner. We had not been ten minutes at the fair—we had only time to go into one show, and come out—we landed from the water, at there o'clock—I had a pint of beer, and then we got to the fair, and were locked up at half-past three o'clock.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18360613-1622]
1622. EDRICK BEACHER and WILLIAM LAWRENCE were indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 1 handkerchief value 1 s., 6 d., the goods of Samuel Maynard, from his person.
WILLIAM DYKE ( police-constable R 193) I was in Deptford fair with James Wild, on the 30th of May—I saw the prisoner Beacher, go behind the prosecutor with the other prisoner, and take the handkerchief from the prosecutor pocket, and then push into the crowed—Lawrence was close to him when he took the handkerchief, and it was passed in some way from Beacher to Lawrence, and was found on him; but I did not see it found, as he fell on the ground and kicked very much.
JAMES WILD ( police-constable R 141.) I was with Dyke at Deptford fair between eleven and twelve o'clock at night—I saw both the prisoners standing behind the prosecutor—I saw Beacher pass something to Lawrence—I took Lawrence, and found this handkerchief in the flap of his trowsers—he threw himself down, and began to kick and bite very mach.
SAMUEL MAYNARD . I live at Layton-stone-hill, and am a waiter. I was in the fair looking at a show—this handkerchief if mine.
Beacher I was selling something in the fair, and saw a piece of work—I went up, and was taken—I did not have the handkerchief at all. Lawrence. He told the Magistrate he did not see the handkerchief come from the man's pocket—I picked it up, and did not steal it—nobody owned it, and I kept it myself.
WILLIAM DYKE . No, I told the Magistrate he was the person who received it, but the other too it—they were standing close to me and they were seized directly—the fair was very thin at that time.
JURY. Q. You said they pushed into the crowd? A. Yes, but the fair was getting thin—there were perhaps one hundred persons before them, but none behind.
BEACHER— GUILTY . Aged 14.
LAWRENCE— GUILTY . Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18360613-1625]
1625. EDWARD BURN was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of May, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the of Thomas Fauntleroy, from his person.
THOMAS FAUNTLEROY . I live at Potter's-fields, and am ivory-merchant. On Tuesday, the 13th of May, I was in Tooley-street, and felt a tug at my coat pocket—I turned round suddenly and caught the prisoner's hand enclosing my handkerchief—I took it from him—I caught his hand and the handkerchief and the tail of my coat together—the handkerchief was all outside the pocket—it was gone from person, because my coat pocket dropped, and I took the handkerchief his hand.
Prisoner. I told you a boy that went along took it—you know well it was not me took the handkerchief—I told you it was another boy—you would not go after him Witness. He told me it was put into his hand by another boy—he was close behind me.
SAMUEL JAMES HALEY . I took charge of the prisoner, and have the handkerchief.
Prisoner. Q. Have you ever known me to dishonest in your life? Witness. I have known him the last six or seven years residing on the spot—I never knew any thing against him—it is some length of time since I knew him to be in any service—he was then a pot-boy.
Prisoner's Defence. I am innocent—I was going for some soap for my mother at the same time.
GUILTY . Aged 14— Recommended to mercy by the prosecutor.
Confined One Month, and Whipped.
[trialAccount t18360613-1626]
1626. THOMAS WARD and JOSEPH STONE were indicted for stealing, on the 21st of May, 3 skewers, 20 s., of Jean Baptiste Alary, from his person.
JEAN BAPTISTE ALARY . I am a cook, and live in Park-street, Grosvenor-square. On Saturday, the 21st of May, I was, I was on my return from Fetcham-park in a wagon—it was half-past ten o'clock when I arrived at Epson—it was about one o'clock in the morning when I lost my skewers—I cannot tell where I was, or in what part of the road—there were many person in the carriage—they stopped at a public-house—I got into a wagon—the prisoners were, there, but not when I left Epsom, they came in
afterwards—I found myself pushed about by them—I meant to walk on foot—I had my portmanteau, when I arrived at Epson—I could not get any coach—I saw one cart, and in that cart five or six gentlemen—they said, "You go to London?"—I said, "Yes, what time do you think you will take to arrive?"—they said, "Five or six hours"—I said, "How much you charge?"—he said, "1 s. 6 d."—they said, "Where is your portmanteau?"—I gave it to him—I got in and took my seat—they stopped a quarter of an hour at one public-house—it was about midnight—the cart stopped again—all the people got down except me—they said, "Come with me"—I said, "No, thank you"—they go in the public-house, and then they come in again in three quarters of an hour—that two prisoners came by my side—I was sleepy—in half an hour I felt my pocket touched—I thought these men would have something from my pocket—a few minutes after I felt the skewers gone from my pocket—after that these men touched my pocket again, and said, "You can sing an Italian song?"—these two left, and the man changed his seat, and squeezed me as much as possible—after that I slept a little, and felt his hand come to my side—I took my coat tail up, and the other made all possible movement to move my hand, and one knocked my head once or twice—I said, "Will you be quiet if you please? I pray you leave me alone—I don't want to play with you"—they laughed, and said many disagreeable words, and squeezed my legs—I complained many times, but it was useless—they did not understand—during that time we had passed one gate, where the carriage paid the duty—they stopped at that time, and then went on again—I called the carter to stop the cart—he said nothing—he smoked his pipe, and I knocked him—he was sleepy all the night—he says, "That is very bad; you make very bad manners with that gentlemen"—then I get over the cart to go and get my portmanteau—he would not let me have it—I said, "You must let me have it"—I cannot tell the precise time when I lost my skewers—it was about one o'clock in the morning—the two prisoners were by me when I lost them—they did not push me before they left—I suppose they came to London—they stopped till I got down, but I stopped about half-road—I got my hat and portmanteau, and went to find the policeman—Stone's hand was on any pocket to get my watch out, but the skewers taken before that—I was sensible they were taken from me—Stone took them, I am certain—when they were out I was much more comfortable—I was not so squeezed—the other prisoner knocked my head to give him facility—I was not knocked at all before the skewers were taken—I was squeezed before I felt them go.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How many persons where in this cart or wagon? A. About nine or ten—I am sure of the number—they were all playing, and asking me to sing—I saw these men searched—the skewers were not found—I saw two men run away these the wagon.
Ward. There were three man sky-larking with him, and they knocked his hat into the road—he got out to get his hat, and then two man got out, and said they would walk—one of them pulled out some skewers, and I said, "Let me look at one of them"—said, "The policeman and three men are coming, "and then he got out and ran away; and when they came and took us, this man and I were both sitting together, and knew nothing about it—there was a person, of the name of Halliday, said he saw the Jews take the things out of the man's pocket.
GEORGE WOODS ( police-constable V 112.) I took the prisoners into custody
on Clapham Common—it was then a quarter before four o'clock in the morning—Ward was down, Stone was in the wagon—I charged them with robbing the prosecutor of three silver skewers—I found nothing on Ward but a knife, and nothing on the other but some money, that was given up.
Cross-examined Q. Were you told of three men that ran away from the wagon? A. I heard there were two—I did not try to pursue them—my brother constable did, but he did not catch them—he did not bring any man back—I saw from seven to eight person in the wagon.
JAMES HALLIDAY . I was in this wagon—I saw the prosecutor and the two prisoners there, and several others, but they are not present—I recollect three men getting out of the front of the van—they kept putting my hat over my eyes, and the prosecutor's too—there was another young man sitting with the prisoner—I saw them do nothing, barring having three silver skewers in their hands, passing them from one to another—I turned my back to them, and was afraid to them, and was afraid to turn round, as I was threatened to have my head punched —the one that kept hitting me over my head got out first, and two others after him—the prosecutor's hat was knocked off, and I called to the wagoner to stop—he stopped and gave it him, and he got out; and then one of them put his hand round him, and he a kind of black book in his hand; and after that I saw the skewers handing about—the two prisoners had them in their hands—I do not know what became of them—the officer came up in an hour, I should think—the prosecutor got out, and went a different road, and then I saw the skewers—I expected the man had been robbed of them—I would not go away; and then the officer came up and the prosecutor, and said that he had been robbed of three silver skewers.
GEORGE WOODS re-examined. Q. What money did you find on either of the prisoners? A. I found 18 d. and a four-penny piece on Stone, and on the other two knives and a box, but no money.
MR. PHILLIPS to JAMES HALLIDAY. Q. How many men ran away from the wagon? A. There—it was one of the three I saw with a black book, and taking something from the prosecutor—I do not know whether every body in the van might have seen the skewers—I had my back turned to them—the three fellows ran off—they were not pursued by the prosecutor and the policeman—I sent them after them—I do not knew how many person there were in the wagon—I should think twelve.
COURT. Q. How long before you got to Clapham Common did you see the skewers? A. Between two and three o'clock—I cannot tell the name of the place.
Ward. This man said he saw the man take them from the gentleman, and Stone said, "Let me look," and he did, and said, "Here comes the man and the officer," and then these three men got out.
Witness. I saw the man that ran away had something black in his hand, after that I saw the skewers handed about—I said I saw the man have his arms round the prosecutor—he had something black is his hand.
JURY. Q. Did you see them in any body's hands, but the prisoners? A. No—that was before those men ran away.
MR. PHILLIPS to JEAN BAPTISTE ALARY. Q. Were these skewers in paper? A. I had a leather memorandum book which I lost.
COURT. Q. Was that in the same pocket as you last your skewers from? A. No—it was some before that I lost the skewers—the
prisoner Ward had pushed me after my skewers were gone, but not before I they both squeezed me very much—they were silver skewers, about seven inches long.
Ward. It was dark when the three men knocking his hat off—I never laid my hand on him—there were fifteen or twenty men is front, and we all sat on one another's lap—I could not keep my seat.
Stone. Halliday, at the time I had the skewer in my hand, asked me to let him look at it—I said I could not.
JAMES HALLIDAY . I did not mention a word to him—I sat quite at the head of the wagon—I had no conversation with any one, except the one who offered to punch my head.
COURT. Q. Did you understand to what place the prisoner were to go by the wagon? A. I did not.
Ward. I have driven my father's wagon three or four years—the three man were Jews—I thought they were selling them about the country.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18360613-1627]
1627. ELIZA BAILEY was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of May, 1 shift, value 1 s.; 1 napkin, value 6 d.; 1 towel, value 6 d.; 1 saucer value 3 d.; 1 1/2 ounce weight of tobacco, value 6 d.; and 1 ounces weight of stone-blue, value 2 d.; the goods of John Edward Owen Sheet, her master.
JOHN EDWARD OWEN SHAW . I live in James's-street, Westminster-road. The prisoner came into our service to nurse my wife on the 4th of May, and remained until the 16th, when, in consequence of suspicion, I sent for a policeman who searched her box in my presence—it was a kind of bend-box—she handed it to him to search—he found in it a napkin, a china saucer, two packages of tobacco, and stone-blue, which were articles I sold in my shop—I afterwards went to Eaton-street, Lambeth, and found a shift belonging to my wife, and a towel—I know the towel to he mine—the prisoner said at the station-house, that she lodged at No.18, Eaton-street, and that was where we found things—her husband searched the drawer there, and produced them.
Prisoner. The napkin is mine, I have it four years—I know nothing of the saucer. Witness. I can swear to the napkin by the name of "Rinfold" being on it—it is nearly erased—we have seen them about for years with this mark on them—I bought them of a person in the King's Bench three or four years' ago—they were pawned at Fleming's, in Fleet-market, and I took them out—the prisoner went home once during the time she was in our service, and could have taken things to her lodging—I found several packages of mine lying about her room, but the things that had been in them were gone.
Prisoner. I did not know the shift was hers—I took it home by mistake, thinking it was one of my own.
JAMES CUDDY ( police-constable L 20.) I was sent for on the 16th of May, and the prosecutor charged the prisoner with stealing, a shift—I asked her if she had a box—she said, had a bit of a cap-box up-stairs—I looked her up—Mr. Shaw drew her box from under the bed, and in it I found the saucer, napkin, packages of tobacco, and stone-blue—she gave her address, "No. 18, Eaton-street, New-cut"—we went there—the landlady said the prisoner husband was out—she sent for him—he came, and
I told him what I wanted—I went into the room, and in searching a chest of drawers, I found this shift and towel.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 25— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.
Before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18360613-1628]
1628. MARY HAGGERTY was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of April, 1 bonnet, value 1 s.; and 1 shawl, value 1 s.; the goods Mary Ann Lee, in the dwelling-house of Eliza Thompson; and that she afterwards did burglariously break out of the said dwelling-house.
MARY ANN LEE . I am servant to Miss Eliza Thompson, who lives in William-street, Waterloo-road. I do not know the parish—house only keeps one servant—I have seen the prisoner as servant at No. 1, but she is no acquaintance of mine—she came to mistress's house on the 25th of April, between seven and eight o'clock in the evening—mistress was out—she knocked at the door, and asked if there was a servant wanted—I said, "Yes," and asked her to come in and wait till mistress came in—(I was going to leave—she sat down, and I fell asleep about nine o'clock)—mistress did not come in till twelve o'clock—I awoke between ten and eleven o'clock and she was then gone—when I fell asleep we were both in the parlourshe was sitting in a chair—I did not miss my bonnet and shawl till next morning—they had hung behind the kitchen door, down stairs—there was nobody in the house besides me and the prisoner—when I went to sleep, the street-door was shut and latched, but not bolted—it was on the when I awoke.
Prisoner. Q. When I went to the house she was doors from her own house—did not I wash up plates and dishes for you, anti sew the skirt of a gown on to a body? Witness. A. It is false—the bonnet and shawl were in the house—I had seen them recently before—these are them
JOHN COLLISON . I am a policeman. On the 26th of April, Lee came to the station-house, and gave information of losing property of her own—I found the prisoner in Tower-street, Waterloo-road, on the 11th of May, and found the bonnet on her head—I was looking for her nearly three weeks—I received the shawl from a man who is not here—the mistress would prosecute, and the Magistrate bound the servant over.
GUILTY of stealing only. Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18360613-1629]
1629. HENRY GREENWELL was indicted for that he, on the 28th of April, in and upon James East, Unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously, did make an assault, and did stab and cur him on the left side of his body, with intent to kill and murder him.—2nd COUNT, stating is to be with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
JAMES EAST . On the morning of the 28th of April, I was in Morgan's-lane, Bermondsey street—I was employed, with others, in emptying a privy—I was coming out of the house, and heard the ladder thrown from the cart—I put my bucket down in the house and ran out—I saw five men near the cart, dressed in sailor's clothes—we asked them they did that for—they said, because they liked it and they would do it again if they liked—the prisoner said that—I am sure he is the man—he made a blow at me—I had not made a blow at him first—my partners ran to save, and then Hagger pitched into him, and then I and Green well began fighting—we had three rounds—he then put his hand into his pocket—I did not him take the knife out,
but I saw him open it right before me, and he stabbed me in the left side—my master came out and shoved him off me, and he ran away, hallooing "Police" several times, and Hagger followed him—this was after we had fought two rounds—I was on the ground when he opened the knife—with one knee up and the other down—he had run after me and pushed me down, and then presented the knife at me and stabbed me in the left side of my body while I was on the ground—he was on both his knees, kneeling on me with one knee on my leg, and I had one foot on the ground—the other knee was kneeling—as soon as he opened the knife he rushed it into me—my master picked me up—I told him the prisoner had stabbed me, and then he ran after him—I can swear I did not ran away after he opened the knife—I could not stir—I laid on the ground till he stabbed me.
Q. Then he never ran after you with the knife open in his hand? A. Yes, he did after the second round—he opened the knife and ran after me with it open his hand—he then knocked me down—he ran seven or eight yards before he overtook me—I went into a public-house, tied a handkerchief round my wound, and was taken to the hospital directly—I have been there seven weeks—I am not yet out of the hospital for goods.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did not you swear at the police-office that you were down on the ground, and that he drew his knife and stabbed you on the ground? A. No, 1 said when he pulled out his knife I got up and ran a few yards, and he overtook me, knocked me down. and stabled me—I did get up before my master picked me up—the prisoner had not been fighting with any body besides me—my uncle did not knock him down—I swear that the prisoner was on me on the ground, and my uncle shoved him off me—he picked me up, seeing me vary faint—after I came too I told him he had stabbed me, and he ran after him—the prisoner called out "Police" several times—he was the first person that called "Police"—I had no knife—we had been eating our supper just before—but we had the publican's knives.
NOT GUILTY .
The prisoner was afterwards convicted on an indictment, charging the above offence as an assault.
Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Justice Patteson.
[trialAccount t18360613-1630]
1630. JAMES WILEY was indicted for stealing, on the 31st of May, at Clapham, I gelding, price 5 l. the property of John Kirby.
JOHN KIRBY . I live at Balaam-hill, Streatham. In May last I had brown gelding—I sent it to be shod on Tuesday, the 31st, by my lad, to Page, the farrier, and it was then turned on Clapham Common—my lad is not here—I generally kept it in my stable, but when it is not work it goes on the Common for an hour or two, and generally comes homes of itself in the evening—it was to be turned on the common after it was shod—I did not see it again till the following Tuesday, when I saw it is a cab down at my place—it was the same horse—I gave two guineas reward for bringing it home—the value of the horse is 5 l.,—I gave 10 l. for it twelve or fourteen years ago.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Do you mean to say could get 5 l. for it? A. Yes—it is an old horse—it would sell in Smithfield for 5 l.—it was sent at twelve o'clock to he shod.
RICHARD THORNTON . I live at Clapham. I have known the prosecutor for years, and know his horse—I saw it on the 31st of May, about
a quarter before nine o'clock in the evening, on Clapham Common, at the top of the Common gate, by Mr. Beacket's—I saw it attempt to drink at the pool—some boys were fishing there, and they drove it away—he made another attempt—that was all saw.
Cross-examined Q. Was that near the road? A. Not a hundred yards from it.
HENRY PAGE . I am a farrier. I should the horse—it was brought to me about twelve o'clock, on the 31st by the prosecutor's lad and he took it away about three o'clock—I saw it again about a quarter before ten o'clock that evening, going along past the yard leading to my house—I live a little way down the lane, near the Bedford Arms—there was a man on it, with a halter—it was on the road towards London—I do not know who the man was—I only saw his back—I bad known the horse for twelve of fourteen years, having been in the habit of shoeing him—I am quite sure it was the same horse.
Cross-examined Q. Was it a light night? A. Not very—it was not a tall stout man on the horse—it was a slight man, I should say—I only had him in view as he passed, for a minute or two, and saw it was the prosecutor's horse—I thought there was something the matter with his other horse, and he had sent for this to fetch his cart.
ROBERT FAWCET . I am one of the toll-collectors at Kennington-gate, I know Kirby's horse perfectly well—I saw it on the evening of the 31st of May, about ten o'clock, pass through the gate towards London—there was a man riding it—I could not tell whether he had a saddle, bridle, or halter, or not—I do not know who the man was.
Cross-examined Q. He paid the toll, suppose? A. Not to me, but to my fellow-servant—there are three lamps at the toll-house—I should not think he could see his features, unless he had occasion to take notice of him—it was very light—if I liked to looked at a man's face I could see it very well—the man who took the toll was at the gate—he has never been examined—I told the Magistrate that another man had received the toll from him, but that he knew no more of it than I did.
SAMUEL EKINS . I am servant to Mr. Clark in Brand-yard, Drummond-street, Euston-square—Mrs. Theobalds, his sister, lives with him. On the night of the 31st of May, between ten and eleven o'clock my mistress desired me to go down to let somebody in—I went found the prisoner there, on a horse—I knew him before—he had a bridle, but no saddle—I asked him what he had got, a donkey or a jackass—he said, "Why, I have got a horse"—I took him into the stable—he said, "I brought it for Charley, " meaning my master; "I brought it for him to work till I get my license"—he wanted a cab license—he asked me what I thought of it—I looked at the horse's mouth, and said, "It is a very old one," and would not do him much good—that was all that passed—I put the horse in the stable for the night—Mr. Clark used him next morning in his cab —I think he used about four times—he took him our last Monday week, and did not came home till Tuesday afternoon, and the horse never came back—it was the same horse as Wiley brought.
Cross-examined Q. Was it not on Wednesday night all this took place? A. No; Tuesday—I have always said it was Tuesday—I was not at the police-office when the prisoner made a statement—the 31st was on a Tuesday—it was the last day of May—I know that by looking at the almanack—I looked at it a day or two after the horse was stolen—I had not seen a handbill describing as lost on the 31st of May, before I looked at
the almanack—I heard it was lost on the 31st of May, before I looked at the almanack—I said the horse was brought to me on the Tuesday before, I looked, to make sure of it—I have known the prisoner about eighteen months—he has been a cab -man, and had a license of his own, and has got one now, I believe—he lived in London Mews—I did not know where to direct the officers to find him—I cannot tell where he kept his cab —I drove for Clark—Clark did not want a cab -horse—the horse went out about four times—it was always in the night—our can works in the day as well as sight, but it was a shabby-looking horse—it did not go out every night—it was in our possession seven or eight days—it was found on Tuesday morning.
MARY THEOBALD . I am Clark's sister, and live in Brand's-yard. I have known the prisoner two years—I recollect his bringing the horse to our place on Tuesday evening, the 31st of May, between ten and eleven o'clock—he asked me to let him leave it there—he had brought it for my brother to work till he got his number—I asked Ekins to take it into the stable—I recollect it was Tuesday, the 31st of May, because my brother took it out on the 1st of June, at night—I am sure of that—I saw the horse the night it came, but did not see the colour till next morning—the prisoner asked me what I supposed he gave for it—I said, "About 5 l."—he said he gave 6 l. that was the night he left it.
Cross-examined Q. I suppose you did not believe him when he said he gave 6 l.? A. I did give it a thought—my brother always works at night, and the man in the morning—I was not before the Magistrate when the prisoner said any thing—I was at Union-hall last Friday about it—I was the first that was called in—I did not hear the other witness say it was Tuesday—the horse went out again on Thursday night Saturday night, and Monday night—I know that, because we put it down—my brother only keeps two horses.
CHARLES CLARK . I am a cab -man. I first saw the horse on Wednesday morning, the 1st of June in my stable, between eight and nine o'clock—I used him in my cab —on the following Monday night I had it in my cab, and drove a person to Clapham—they told me to stop against Kirby's house; and Kirby came out, claimed the horse, and gave me in charge—I gave it up after he swore to it.
Cross-examined Q. Did not Kirby tell you when he had lost his horse? A. He said he lost it that day week—I was out when it came to my stable.
RICHARD GOLDING . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner on Tuesday, the 7th of June, in Euston-mews, Euston-square—I told him it was for a horse stolen from Clapham-common on the 31st of last May—he said, "I bought the horse"—I asked him. "When?—he said, "On Thursday, the 2nd of this month, and gave 3 l. 10 s. for it"—I asked what he did in the evening when he bought it—he said he took it to Clark's stable—I took him into custody.
Cross-examined Q. You found him where he had his cab? A. Not that I know of—he was cleaning horses—I had information from Clark, that if I went to Drummond-crescent, I should find him; but I did not find him there—I went to the stable, and saw him rubbing horses down—I said to him, "Does a man named Wiley live here?—he said, "Do you want him?"—I said, "Yes"—he put his coat on, and said, "I will go and find him"—it was what I heard at Drummond-crescent that took me to where I found the prisoner—I am sure the word "Thursday" was
mentioned in bringing him to the station-house at Clapham—nobody was present—this was on Tuesday—he said he took it to Clark's the night he bought it, and he said he bought it on Thursday—I was particular in asking him whether it was Thursday or Tuesday—he said he bought it of a man at Cow-cross, who gave his name as Jones.
Prisoner's Defence I bought the horse the horse in Cow-cross, for 3 l. 10 s., of a a little man, on Wednesday week last.
(Thomas Price, tea-street, Dorset-square; William Diggins, corn-chandler, Brewer-street, Somers-town; and William Burdett, cab -man, Grevill-street, Somers-town, gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Life.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360613-1631]
1631. EDWARD STRAWSON was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of May, 1 account-book value 1 s.; 1 bag, value 3 s.; and 4 pairs of shoes, value 18 s.; the goods of William Gooding, his master: and 1 account book, value 1 s.; 3 pairs of shoes, value 13 s. 6 d.; and 1 pair of half-boots, value 6 s. 6 d.; the goods of George Gooding; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM GOODING . I live in St. Paul's-place, Walworth-common, and am a shoemaker. The prisoner was about three weeks in my service—on Saturday morning, the 21st of May, I lost four pairs of shoes—He absented himself at the same time—I made inquiries, and found the shoes at the pawnbroker's—this is one pair of mine—this is my brother's—I lost am account-book, and my brother one, also a bag, a pair of boots.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Are you not carrying on business with your brother? A. No; he only lodges with me—I am quite sure this pair is mine, I had seen them on the Saturday morning—this pair is my workmanship, and this pair is my brother's—I do not keep a shop—only one person employs me—he would have a number of pairs of shoes manufactured by me, and they would resemble this pair—there is nothing that would distinguish them from this pair.
COURT. Q. How long before you missed these two pairs had you seen them? A. Twenty minutes before I missed them—the prisoner absented himself before breakfast.
JOSIAH WILLIAM CLARKE . I am a pawnbroker. I produce this pair of shoes claimed by the prosecutor—they were pledged by the prisoner on the 21st of May, for 1 s. 6 d —I cannot tell what time of the day.
Cross-examined Q. Are you quite sure of his person? A. Yes.
ROBERT COX . I am a pawnbroker, living with Mr. Farmer, in Blackfriars-road. This and the other pair were brought by the prisoner about twenty minutes past nine o'clock on the morning of the 21st of May—he said they were for his brother, George Gooding, whose name is in them.
JOHN LYONS ( police-constable L 161.) I took the prisoner, and found these two books and a riding belt on him.
WILLIAM GOODING re-examined This book is mine, the other is my brother's—this is the book in which my employer puts what I take.
JAMES SYMONS ( police-constable P 17) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the peace, at Newington ( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 14— Transported for Seven Years.
Sixth Jury before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360613-1632]
1632. GEORGE SMITH was indicted for misdemeanor.
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18360613-1633]
1633. ROBERT COLE was indicted for embezzlement to which he pleaded.
GUILTY .. Aged 16.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18360613-1634]
1634. ROBERT HART was indicted for a misdemeanor.
NOT GUILTY .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JULY 4, 1836.
